HIGHLIGHTS P. L. 874--Maintenance and Operation 

$75,000,000 was appropriated for fiscal year 1955. 

The number of local school districts aided increased for the fifth straight year to total 2,683.

The pupils in attendance approximated 6,000,000 in these school districts, of which 16 percent were federally connected. 

The $71,472,449 of net entitlements for Federal funds for these school districts constituted 5 percent of their total budgets. 

Net entitlements of $67,012,059 resulted from the attendance of 874,658 pupils who either resided on Federal property or with parents employed on Federal property or both. 

In addition, net entitlements of $2,746,526 resulted from attendance of 17,170 pupils representing a sudden and substantial increase in school attendance of federally connected pupils. 

Payments of $3,603,969 were made for federally operated schools on 34 installations, chiefly military bases, on behalf of 15,784 pupils in attendance. 

The number of tax-exempt properties claimed for payment purposes totalled 3,263. 

P.L. 815--Construction 

Since 1950, appropriations have totalled $609 million (including $70 million appropriated for fiscal year 1955).

The number of school construction projects on which Federal funds were reserved totalled 2,898 since 1950 for approximately 1,350 local school districts. 

Some 26,000 classrooms to house an estimated 774,000 pupils have been approved at a total Federal cost of $577,012,498. 

In addition to the Federal funds allocated since 1950, school districts have added $210,000,000 (exclusive of sites) to the construction projects to house federally connected and other pupils without available school facilities. 

1,653 projects have been completed; 684 are under construction. 

143 projects costing $50,484,122 to house 43,787 pupils have been constructed on 

Federal property, chiefly military installations.

FIFTH ANNUAL REPORT, 1955 Public Laws 874 and 815 INTRODUCTION This is the Fifth Annual Report of the U.S. 

Commissioner of Education on the adminis tration of Public Law 874, as amended, and Public Law 815, as amended. These Acts authorize Federal financial assistance to local educational agencies affected by activities of the Federal Government. The report reviews accomplishments under both Acts as well as the amendments to the Acts which were effective for the 1955 fiscal year. It also presents a complete summary of receipts and disbursements and statistical tables reflecting allotments to various school districts. 

The policy of the Federal Government in enacting this legislation is stated in one of the Acts as follows: 

"In recognition of the responsibility of the United States for the impact which certain Federal activities have on the local educational agencies in the areas in which such activities are carried on, the Congress hereby declares it to be the policy of the United States to provide financial assistance (as set forth in the following sections of this Act) for those local educational agencies upon which the United States has placed financial burdens by reason of the fact that-- "1. The revenues available to such agencies from local sources have been reduced as the result of the acquisition of real property by the United States; or "2. Such agencies provide education for children residing on Federal property; or "3. Such agencies provide education for children whose parents are employed on Federal property; or 

4. There has been a sudden and substantial increase in school attendance as the result of Federal activities."

Public Law 874, authorizing payments to federally affected school districts for current operating expenses, was extended and amended by P.L. 248 in the first session of the 83d 

Congress. Public Law 732 of the second session of the 83d Congress again amended P.L. 

874 by deferring for the 1955 fiscal year the absorption provision incorporated into the Law by P.L. 248. Public Law 382 of the first session of the 84th Congress again amended P.L. 

874 by deferring for an additional year, the 1956 fiscal year, the 3-percent absorption provision, making other minor changes, and extending the Act until June 30, 1957. 

The school construction program (Title II 

of P.L. 815) originally had an effective period of 2 years to June 30, 1952. As a result of continued Federal impacts, the first session of the 83d Congress passed P.L. 246 in August 

1953 extending the school construction act (P.L. 815) for two additional years until June 

30, 1954, and made substantial changes in its provisions. Again, in the second session of the 83d Congress, P.L. 246 was extended without change to June 30, 1956, by the passage of P.L. 731. The first session of the 84th

Congress again made minor amendments to P.L. 815, some of which apply retroactively. 

Public Law 246 also extended from June 30, 1953, to June 30, 1954, the time in which Congress was authorized to appropriate funds to pay entitlements established by applicants under the original Act which had not been paid by earlier appropriations, but limited the amount that could be paid to any school district to 70 percent of its unpaid entitlement and further limited the total that could be appropriated for this purpose to $55,000,000. Public 

Law 357 of the second session of the 83d 

Congress, approved in May 1954, appropriated the full $55,000,000 that had been authorized for this purpose and required that all projects under this authorization be approved by March 1, 1955. A number of other projects approved under the original P.L. 815 and its first extension by P.L. 246 were still active during the 1955 fiscal year. 1

SUMMARY OF ACCOMPLISHMENTS A. PUBLIC LAW 874 

The fiscal year ending June 30, 1955 was the first year that applicants were paid under section 3 of the Act for the attendance of federally connected children during the preceding year. It also was the first year in which no entitlements were authorized and no payments made under the former subsections 4(b), 3(e), and 3(f). Finally, it was the first year in which the new eligibility requirements under subsection 4(a) applied. The number of eligible applications and the amount of assistance for which districts were eligible increased for the fifth straight year.

Applications were filed for the 1954-55 school year by 2,832 local educational agencies in all 48 States and Alaska and Hawaii. (See table 1.) The Commissioner determined that 149 of these applicants were ineligible, or had no net entitlement under the provisions of the Act; and that 2,683 were eligible and entitled to an estimated net total of $71,472,443 in Federal funds under all sections of the Act, except section 6. (See tables 3 and 5.) In the preceding fiscal year, there were 2,524 applicants eligible for $69,130,473.

Since it is estimated that the sum of all entitlements will exceed the funds appropriated for fiscal year 1955 by approximately $165,000, it will be necessary to prorate final payments to eligible districts at 99.5 percent of their entitlements after paying section 6 applicants in full. 

The entitlements for the 2,683 eligible school districts were based on 92,868 children in average daily attendance who resided on Federal property with a parent employed on such property (the 3(a) category); 781,790 who either lived on Federal property or lived with a parent employed on Federal property (the 

3(b) category); and 17,170 whose attendance constituted an increase in average daily attendance resulting from activities of the Federal Government over the average daily attendance during the preceding year (the 4(a) category).

Entitlement for children claimed under the 3(b) category, those who lived on Federal property or lived with a parent employed on Federal property, but not both, accounted for a little less than $56 million, or almost 74 percent of the total entitlements computed under the Act. These funds were paid to 

2,569 school districts. A total of 365 school districts claimed children living on Federal property with a parent employed thereon, the 3(a) category, and were eligible for about $15 million, or about 20 percent of the total. 

Payments made to these local educational agencies on account of Federal property pursuant to other Federal laws deductible from gross entitlement pursuant to P.L. 874, as amended, amounted to $4,190,958. The majority of these deductible payments were payments in lieu of taxes on federally owned public housing, taxes paid on Wherry Housing projects, and leasehold interest taxes paid by private individuals on Federal property held under lease. The total amount deducted was less in the 1955 fiscal year than in the preceding year due primarily to the reduction in payments in lieu of taxes as additional Federal housing projects were transferred to local or private ownership.

In 30 instances the U.S. Commissioner of 

Education delegated authority and made funds available to another agency (local educational or Federal) under section 6 of the Act for the education of 15,784 children in average daily attendance residing on 34 Federal properties at a cost to the Federal Government of $3,603,969. These arrangements were necessary in situations where the State or local educational agencies were prohibited from expending tax revenues for the education of such children, or where no local educational agency was able to provide suitable free public education. There was an increase of 5 such section 6 projects over the preceding year, 3 of which were the result of the January 12, 1954 order of the Secretary of Defense that no new school located on a military base could open on a racially segregated basis or an established segregated school continue such basis after September 1, 1955; the other 

2 were new projects for Mammoth Cave Na tional Park (Kentucky) and Wake Island. 

on Entitlements under all sections of the Act in fiscal year 1955 totalled $75,164,619. Payments on these entitlements, made on account of approximately 910,000 federally connected children in average daily attendance, actually benefited more than 6 million children enrolled in the school districts receiving assistance, since Federal funds are available for all current operating expenses of the school districts for all pupils. Federal payments constituted on the average about 5 to 6 perthe total operating budgets in the eligible districts, while the federally connected children counted for entitlement purposes constituted approximately 16 percent of the total number of school children in the cent eligible districts. The districts receiving assistance enrolled about one-fifth of all elementary and secondary school children in the Nation. 2

There were 32 eligible applicants under activity and had incurred an undue financial section 2 entitled to a net total of $309,535 in Federal funds on account of the acquisition by the Federal Government of formerly taxable real property in the districts since 1939. 

The number of eligible applicants increased slightly in fiscal year 1955 over the 1954 fiscal year.

School districts which experienced a sudden and substantial increase in attendance resulting from Federal activities in the current year could make application for first-year assistance under section 4 of the Act. There were 269 districts which made such application. Children who could be counted for firstyear assistance under this section include two classes: (1) pupils whose parents moved into the district to work in plants having Federal contracts (but who are not employed on or living on Federal property); and (2) pupils who represent an increase in the current year under section 3 (which pupils are not eligible to be counted under section 3 in the current year but are in the following year). Fiscal year 1955 was the first year in which the second class of children was counted under this section; previously the second class of children was eligible to be counted for entitlement in the current year under section 3 of the Act. There were 89 districts which met the eligibility requirements of section 4 for first-year assistance. Districts were eligible if they had a 5-percent increase over the preceding year in attendance due to Federal burden because of the expenses accompanying this increase. The amount to which the districts were entitled was $2,746,526.

There were 159 more school districts applying for assistance under sections 2, 3, and 4, and 159 more school districts eligible for assistance in the 1955 fiscal year than in the preceding year.

A study made in 1955 comparing federally affected school districts and other school districts indicates that school districts receiving P.L. 874 assistance are growing in attendance at about twice the rate of other districts throughout the country. At the same time, per-pupil expenditures in federally affected districts have been increasing at a faster pace than the national average increase in recent years. In fiscal year 1951 the average per-pupil cost in all federally affected districts was $178; in fiscal year 1955 it amounted to $241.50, an increase of approximately 36 percent. The Federal contribution has increased on the average from $58 per child in fiscal year 1951 to approximately $75 per child in 

1955, or an increase of 29 percent. 

Federal financial assistance under P.L. 874 has aided eligible districts to keep pace with the increasing needs of rapidly expanding school population. At the same time State and local funds have increased in even greater proportion to meet the growing educational expenses in federally affected districts. 

B. PUBLIC LAW 815: TITLE I 

Public Law 815 (81st Cong.), as originally enacted, included two titles: Title I, which authorized Federal funds to assist States to inventory existing school facilities, to survey the need for construction of additional school facilities, to develop State plans for school construction programs, and to study State and local resources available to meet school facil- 

C. PUBLIC LAW 815: 

STATUS OF LEGISLATION 

June 30, 1952 was the final date on which applications under Title II of the original 

Public Law 815 could be accepted. The Congress later authorized and appropriated funds to pay a part of the cost of eligible projects which had been submitted under this title but which had not been approved because of insufficient funds. ities requirements; and Title II, which authorized Federal funds for construction of school facilities in local educational agencies affected by Federal activities.

During fiscal year 1955 the report of the longrange planning phase of the survey under Title I was completed and prepared for publication. 

TITLES II, III AND IV 

Public Law 246, approved August 8, 1953, extended P.L. 815 from its original ending date of June 30, 1952, to June 30, 1954, to meet the new school-housing needs resulting from Federal activities in that 2-year period.

This extension was authorized by the addition to the Act of Title III, which provided for grants to local educational agencies and construction of schools on Federal property, with eligibility requirements not identical, but similar, to those in Title II of the original Act. 3

P.L. also added Title IV, designed to meet the problem faced by those school districts educating substantial numbers of children living on tax-exempt Indian lands. Title IV had a closing date of June 30, 1955, for project approvals.

In August 1954 Congress extended Title III 

for another 2-year period to June 30, 1956, by the passage of P.L. 731, and in August 1955 

Title IV was extended to June 30, 1956, by the passage of P.L. 382.

The original act and all of the above-mentioned amendments and extensions as in effect during the fiscal year 1955 and the program thereunder will be summarized in this report. 

ACCOMPLISHMENTS UNDER THE ORIGINAL TITLE II 

By June 30, 1952, the final date for the acceptance of applications under Title II, 1,125 school districts had filed claims for assistance under section 202, of which 941 districts were found to be eligible for aid and 

184 ineligible. The 941 eligible school districts submitted applications for approximately 1,600 construction projects requiring $365,000,000 and for 184 reimbursement projects amounting to a little over $27,000,000. 

The requirements for construction of temporary facilities (under section 203) were approximately $5,000,000; and the requirements for school facilities on Federal property (under section 204), of a little over $40,000,000, brought the grand total requirements to approximately $437,000,000.

Congress had appropriated a total of $341,500,000 with which to pay this total of $437,000,000 in valid requests. This necessitated the use of the priority system and administrative decisions on the basis of the relative urgency of need as to which districts would get school construction aid.

Of the total of $341,500,000 appropriated, $291,403,117 was allocated to 740 school districts to construct 1,224 projects. The 

States and communities added approximately $144,500,000 additional, making a total of $435,903,117 for construction of the 1,224 projects. These funds are exclusive of the cost of site and of off-site improvements. 

Funds have been approved to build 420 elementary schools, 164 secondary schools, 587 additions to existing schools, and 51 other projects. (See table 23.) These facilities provided 14,006 equipped classrooms and related facilities containing about 29,000,000 square feet of floor space and provided school facilities for around 408,000 children. As of August 

31, 1955, 1,139 projects were completed and in use and the remaining 84 were rapidly coming to completion. School districts in the 

Territories of Alaska and Hawaii and in all 

States except Delaware, New Hampshire,

Wyoming, and West Virginia received allotments. (See tables 12 and 13.)

A total of $4,342,736 was allotted to provide 20 temporary buildings for approximately 8,833 children in districts where temporary 

Federal impacts occurred. All these buildings are complete. (See table 17.)

The sum of $40,131,735 was allotted to build 99 projects located on Federal property, containing 1,280 classrooms and related facilities to house 36,381 children. All but 15 of these projects were completed and in use as of 

August 31, 1955. (See table 18.)

ACCOMPLISHMENTS UNDER TITLE III 

An appropriation of $70,000,000 was made to carry out the purposes of P.L. 246 for fiscal year 1954 and a cutoff date for the receipt of applications to participate in this first year's appropriation was set at November 24, 1953. This appropriation was not sufficient to meet the claims that were on file by that cutoff date or were expected to be filed by the ending date of the Act, June 30, 1954. An additional $70,000,000 was appropriated on June 2, 1954, for the completion of this program during the 1955 fiscal year. (Of the total appropriation of $140,000,000, $20,000,000 was for construc tion of school facilities under Title IV.) 

In May 1955 Congress appropriated an additional $48,500,000 to carry out P.L. 731 and authorized the use in addition of any available balances remaining from previous ap propriations. Four million dollars of Title II 

balances were transferred for this purpose and approximately $17,300,000 of P.L. 246 funds were unobligated and made available for P.L. 731 projects. In August 1955 Congress appropriated an additional $24,000,000 to finance P.L. 731 projects. This makes a total of $216,500,000 that has been appropriated, including $4 million of Title II balances, for two extensions of P.L. 815 of which $196,500,000 was for Title III and $20,000,000 

for Title IV. 

By August 31, 1955, 1,290 applications had been submitted under the original Title III and its extension requesting funds for the construction of 1,800 projects. As of the same date 785 of these applications were determined to be eligible for assistance, 505 were ineligible, and final action had not been taken on 33 applications. A total of $158,182,056 had been reserved for the construction of 934 projects. 

As of August 31, 1955, 88 Title III projects

had been completed, 389 were under construc- 

ACCOMPLISHMENTS UNDER P.L. 357 tion, and 257 were nearing the construction stage.

The projects for which funds were reserved under this title include approximately 16,000 square feet of floor space and will provide 7,708 classrooms and related facilities to house 230,359 children. (See table 21.)

Twelve projects have been approved in the amount of $1,685,430 to construct temporary school facilities. A total of $10,352,387 was allocated to construct 44 Federal school projects (under section 310) for children who live on Federal property where no local educational agency was able to provide the school facilities.

ACCOMPLISHMENTS UNDER TITLE IV 

Under Title IV, which authorizes construction of facilities primarily for Indian children, 112 school districts applied for assistance by August 31, 1955, of which 80 had been determined to be eligible. As of the same date, $16,102,900 in Federal funds had been allotted to 91 projects in this category to construct school facilities for approximately 18,645 children. Under current regulations, districts have until February 15, 1956, to file applications under this title. Most of these projects have been located in Oklahoma, New Mexico, Montana, Arizona, and Minnesota. (See tables 

19 and 20.)

PAYMENT OF UNPAID ENTITLEMENTS 

One of the provisions of P.L. 246 authorized an appropriation of $55,000,000 in fiscal year 1954 to pay a portion (not to exceed 70 percent) of the cost of eligible projects for which insufficient funds had been appropriated under 

Title II of P.L. 815. This appropriation was accomplished in P.L. 357 of the 83d Congress.

Under P.L. 357 all districts which had unpaid entitlements remaining from Title II were required to file request for payment by October 

1, 1954, with the right to amend their applications by December 31, 1954, to (1) substitute a different project or (2) substitute a reimbursement request based upon construction of the original project. All funds had to be allocated by April 1, 1955. The Act further provided that the $55,000,000 must be prorated among those eligible applicant districts which refiled requests by October 1. It also specified the condition under which applicants could be reimbursed for projects which already had been constructed. 

There were 405 applicants having 503 eligible projects that qualified for unpaid entitlements amounting to $92,411,749. The funds available permitted the payment of 59.38 percent of the amount due each applicant. Applicants requested reimbursements for 307 projects and were paid their pro rata share amounting to $31,443,311. Funds in the amount of $23,368,826 were reserved for 196 new construction projects. LO

FEDERAL PROPERTY 

Federal property is defined in Public Laws 

815 and 874 as "real property...owned...or leased by the United States...not subject to taxation by any State or any political subdivi sion of a State...or by the District of Columbia.'' 

Thus in the administration of these laws it has been necessary to obtain information on the status of those Federal properties claimed by local school systems requesting financial assistance. Information obtained includes the identification of the Federal agency having control of the property and location and acreage of properties owned, leased, or otherwise controlled by the Federal Government, and data on permits, grants, and other exchanges between Federal departments. Properties may be inleased by the Federal Government from private owners and political subdivisions of the State, or outleased by the Federal Government to private owners.

P.L. 874 requires that deductions from computed entitlements to Federal funds must be made for taxes paid, for payments in lieu of taxes, and for other Federal payments made on behalf of Federal properties. Accordingly, information must be obtained showing, for each school district applying for assistance, the amount of any payments that must be deducted. 

These include payments in lieu of taxes on 

Public Housing Administration projects, taxes on Wherry Housing projects, Federal Housing 

Administration projects, Tennessee Valley 

Authority projects, and other projects conducted by the National Government on Federal properties.

Federal payments representing allotments to local educational agencies from United States 

Forestry Reserve funds, Migratory Bird Con servation Act funds, Taylor Grazing Act funds, United States Mineral Lease Royalty funds, or similar funds, are deducted from a school district's entitlement under P.L. 874, only to the extent that a school district claims children connected with the property with respect to which funds are paid. Deductions also are made for possessory or leasehold-interest taxes levied on Federal property outleased by the Federal Government to private contractors. 

During the 1954-55 school year 3,263 eligible Federal properties were claimed by school districts applying for Federal assistance.

These properties contained an estimated 141,027,812 acres of tax-exempt federallyowned and -leased land. This information is shown by States in table 9. 

The following tabulation presents data on the Federal properties claimed by applicants under P.L. 874 for the 1953-54 and 1954-55 school years according to the Federal agency which has jurisdiction of the property: Properties Acres

It will be noted that the Department of the Army has 17.6 percent of the total number of properties and 5.8 percent of the total acreage, as compared with the Air Force which owns 

9.1 percent of the properties and 7.1 percent of the acreage. The Navy, with 15.5 percent of the total number of properties, has 1.3 percent of the total acreage. In other words, the Navy acreage equals approximately one-fifth of the 

Army acreage. At the same time, Public 

Housing Administration has 13.9 percent of the total number of properties and only .01 percent of the total number of acres. 

Under "other" agencies are included properties under the jurisdiction of the Treasury 

Department (Coast Guard); Post Office De partment; Department of the Interior (Bureau of Indian Affairs, Bureau of Reclamation, and 

National Park Service); Department of Agri culture (Forest Service); Civil Aeronautics 

Administration; Atomic Energy Commission;

General Services Administration; Tennessee duplicated properties and of extensive areas 

Valley Authority; and Veterans' Administra tion. The properties owned by these agencies constitute 85.8 percent of the acreage of the 

Federal property claimed. Indian Reserva tions, National Forests, National Parks, and Bureau of Reclamation lands account for the greater portion of the acreage for this group. 

There were 3,263 eligible Federal properties claimed by eligible school districts in the 1954-55 school year as compared with 2,706 in 1953-54. This is an increase of 557 different properties. There has been an increase of 

Federal properties claimed each year. This increase may represent newly acquired Federal properties or old properties that have not previously been claimed by applicants. 

The decrease in acreage for defense agencies is due, principally, to the elimination of not physically connected with the parent installation. No children were claimed from these outlying properties. The decrease in acreage for Public Housing Administration properties is the result of the disposal of housing projects during the school year 1954-55. In 1953-54 

the Public Housing Administration projects were counted by management number which included several individual projects; in 1954-55 the count was made by individual projects, thus accounting for an apparent increase in the number of properties. The increase in acreage in the "Other" agency group may be accounted for by additional properties claimed, particularly National Forests, Public Domain, and Indian Reservations. Many of the claims for school assistance based on Public Domain lands have resulted from the increased activity in uranium prospecting.

PROVIDING FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE FOR MAINTENANCE AND OPERATION OF SCHOOLS UNDER PUBLIC LAW 874 GENERAL 

Public Law 874, enacted in 1950, provides financial aid for maintenance and operation of schools in areas where activities of the Federal Government have created serious school problems. The number of eligible applicants has grown steadily, the amount of entitlements to affected school districts has increased, and Congress has augmented the appropriation each year to meet the increased need. 

The program reflects growth for fiscal year 1955, continuing the pattern of previous years. 

During the 1954-55 school year, the average daily attendance of 891,828 pupils constituted the basis for payments under sections 3 and 4 of the Act, as follows: subsection 3(a), 92,868; 

3(b), 781,790; and 4(a), 17,170. (See table 2.)

Under section 3 of the Act, children become eligible to be counted for Federal assistance in a subsequent year, if other conditions of the Act are met by the applicant district, when they live on Federal property or live with a parent who works on Federal property. Section 4 provides assistance on a current basis for increases in such children and those whose parents are employed by industrial firms having contracts with the Federal Government. 

Therefore, payments are made under these two sections of the Act on the basis of the number of federally connected children in average daily attendance at the school in a designated school year.

Section 2 provides financial assistance, if certain conditions are met, to school districts which have suffered a substantial and continuing financial burden through acquisition by the Federal Government of real property since 1939 and the consequent removal of such realty from the tax base of the districts. 

NUMBER AND ENTITLEMENT OF APPLICANTS 

There were 2,832 applicants under sections 

2, 3, and 4 for aid in 1954-55. This is an increase of 159 over the preceding year. Of this number, 2,683 were eligible, which represents an increase over 1953-54 of 159 in the number eligible. Under section 2, 55 school districts applied for aid; 2,742 under section 3; and 306 under section 4.

The eligible districts were entitled to $71,472,443. Of this amount, $309,535 was paid under section 2, $67,012,059 under section 3, and $4,150,849 under section 4. The average entitlement for 1954-55 was $26,639 which is a decrease from the $26,950 average paid in the prior year.

LOCAL CONTRIBUTION RATE 

The local contribution rate, the rate used to compute entitlements under section 3, is based on current expenditures from local revenue receipts of districts most nearly comparable to that of the applicant in the second preceding year, with a minimum rate equal to one-half the State average per capita cost in 1952-53. 

The average local contribution rate in 1954-55 (based on 1952-53 current expenditures from local receipts of comparable districts or half the State average cost per child) was $147.06.

This is 3 percent higher than the average 195354 local contribution rate of $142.70. The highest average local contribution rate paid in any State for 1954-55 was $247.29 (State of 

Wisconsin), and the lowest was $65.09 (State of Alabama). Forty-two States had average local contribution rates amounting to more than $100 and 6 States had average local contribution rates less than $100. Only 11 States had average local contribution rates in excess of $200 per child. (See table 4.)

CHILDREN LIVING ON FEDERAL INSTALLATIONS 

Under the provisions of section 6 of the Act, the Commissioner may provide education for those children who are living on Federal property and who might be denied access to local school facilities either because State law forbids expending local educational monies for such federally connected children or because the local school districts are, in the judgment of the Commissioner, unable to provide suitable free public education for such children. Such schools are usually located on Federal military installations. 

It was necessary in 30 instances for the Commissioner to make arrangements under section 6 with other agencies to provide free public education to children living on 34 different Federal installations. (See table 7.)

Such education was provided to nearly 16 thousand pupils at a cost of about $3.6 million in 1954-55. 

SERVICES PROVIDED BY OTHER FEDERAL AGENCIES 

Primarily, the services provided by other Federal agencies to applicant districts involved transportation of pupils and custodial

and maintenance services. To cover the value amount of $55,000,000. In April 1955, an addiof such services in 1954-55, $88,207 was deducted from the entitlements of districts applying under section 3. (See table 8.)

APPROPRIATIONS 

The first appropriation for fiscal year 1955 was made by the Congress in July 1954 in the tional $20,000,000 brought the total 1955 appropriation to $75,000,000. This is an increase of $2,650,000 over the amount appropriated for the program in fiscal year 1954 and an increase of $45,919,212 over the amount available for fiscal year 1951, the first year of the program. (See table 6.)

ADMINISTRATION 

From the beginning of the program certain provisions of the law have caused difficulties in administration. One of these is to "determine which school districts within the [applicant's] State are in his [the Commissioner's] judgment most nearly comparable' to the applicant's. Another is to determine whether properties claimed by applicants are Federal within the meaning of the Act. A third is to determine amounts to be deducted from entitlements of applicants.

MOST NEARLY COMPARABLE DISTRICTS 

Most nearly comparable districts are used to determine the rate of the Federal payment for each federally connected child on the assumption that the federally affected applicant district (if its financial base had not been adversely affected by Federal activities) would have been providing, from local sources, an amount per child for public school purposes equivalent to the amount provided by comparable school districts that have not experienced a Federal impact. However, comparability of districts is difficult to determine because of the many variables involved. In a step toward uniformity and objectivity, extensive criteria have been established to aid in the determination of what is or is not a comparable district. For example, comparable districts should be similar to the applicant district in legal classification, number and kind of school population, tax resources, tax effort, and costs of school maintenance and operation. Other factors considered are the teacher-pupil ratio, teachers' salaries, the kind of community (urban, rural, or suburban), and the economic characteristics of the community (industrial, residential, or agricultural).

One solution that has worked well is to group districts with certain class characteristics (size and type of school--elementary or high school), and to determine a local contribution rate for all districts of that grouping in a State. This method of rate determination is believed to be comparatively detached and equitable because of the greater number of districts used to arrive at the necessary figures. Such rates are called "group rates".

FEDERAL PROPERTY 

Although most of the difficulties in obtaining accurate information on Federal properties 373395 - 56 - 2 have been overcome, a few persist and occasionally new ones arise. Nevertheless, the accurate certification of the status each year of thousands of parcels of property acquired for Federal uses requires constant checking and verification. (See table 9.) The applicant frequently refers to Federal property by the name used locally rather than by the legal name making identification of such property difficult to establish. Property sometimes becomes ineligible during the school year. This may decrease or eliminate financial aid to which the district believed it was entitled. 

Some military installations and industrial plants are located on "commingled" properties, defined as properties owned in part by the Federal Government and in part by private owners. However, the applicant, not being aware of the commingled status of the property, may either consider all children eligible or claim no children at all. Such confusion frequently results in the reprocessing of applications claiming such properties. Occasionally, parcels of a large tract of federally owned land are sold to private persons. Particularly is this true of federally constructed wartime housing projects and property claimed by applicants under section 2 of the Act. These transactions are not always known by the applicant or by the Office of Education; thus there is need for constant verification of the status of these properties. Such a condition sometimes results in the disqualification of an applicant district which had previously received an entitlement. 

DEDUCTIONS FROM ENTITLEMENTS 

The Act states that any Federal payment made available to a local educational agency with respect to Federal property under any Act other than P.L. 874 should be deducted from the computed gross entitlement. Such payments include payments in lieu of taxes, shared revenue payments, those payments made directly by the Federal Government, and taxes paid with respect to Federal property or the privately owned improvements located on Federal property regardless of who pays them. 

Federal property payments

Federal property payments and some shared revenue payments are made directly to the

State or its political subdivisions and may or may not be apportioned by these governmental units to individual school districts. Therefore, it is frequently necessary to examine local nonschool records to determine the exact amount of the payment received by the school district in the form of Federal property payments. Pilot

Payments in lieu of taxes, made principally by the Public Housing Administration, are less difficult to ascertain, although districts frequently report in-lieu tax payments made in connection with low-rent housing, which payments, in accordance with the Act, are not deductible. 

Possessory interest taxes

Some but not all States have a possessory or leasehold interest tax. Thus, Wherry Housing located on Federal property is subject to such a taxation in some but not in all States. Wherry tax payments have been made under protest in many States and such taxes may be held in escrow pending court action to determine legality of the payment. Until such funds are actually available to the applicant district for current expenditure purposes, the deduction cannot be made. 

Payments on account of Federal property

Since the passage of P.L. 248, certain Federal payments are deductible only when children living on the Federal land for which the Federal payment is made are claimed by the applicant as a basis for entitlement. These payments are described in subsection 3(e) as those "representing an allotment to the local educational agency from the United States 

Forestry Reserve funds, Taylor Grazing Act funds, United States Mineral Lease Royalty funds, Migratory Bird Conservation Act funds, or similar funds." Difficult administrative problems also continue to arise in determining the extent to which payments from such allotments are available for current-expense purposes and therefore deductible.

Taxes on improvements on Federal property 

Most difficult of all, however, is to determine the amount of taxes paid on improvements on federally owned land, such as forest lands and Indian reservation lands. Such tax payments are deductible from the entitlement of the district which receives the taxes whether or not the district claimed any children living on the federally owned land on which the taxable improvements are located.

It is extremely difficult to determine the amount of taxes paid and eventually available for school purposes on water tanks, power lines crossing over Federal property, utility lines, drift fences, wells and other similar "improvements". Not only must the amount of the tax for school purposes be determined, but a detailed inspection of the records is required to ascertain which district received or should have received the payments. In some instances these taxes have eliminated a district entirely from Federal assistance under P.L. 874, even though none of the children who form the basis for the entitlement lives on the Federal lands where the taxable improvements are located. 

There has been some litigation regarding the authority of the States or their political subdivisions to levy taxes on Wherry Housing projects built on Federal installations. In most 

States, Wherry Housing is now taxed or payments are being made under protest pending final determination. The first session of the 

84th Congress enacted P. L. 388, which provided for a payment in lieu of taxes on properties formerly belonging to the Reconstruction Finance Corporation but now being held by other Federal agencies. This Act will bring in a new group of properties that will have to be checked for deductible payments.

PRORATION OF ENTITLEMENTS UNDER SECTIONS 2, 3, AND 4 

One of the major problems involved in the administration of the program is the proration of entitlements. In case insufficient funds are appropriated in any year, the Act provides in subsection 5(c) that the Commissioner of Education prorate payments on a percentage basis. 

Therefore, it is necessary that the total of all entitlements under sections 2, 3, and 4 be computed in order to determine whether proration is required, and if so, what percentage. 

The deadline date for the submission of final reports is, by regulation, September 30, 3 months following the close of the fiscal year. 

Many of the applicant districts submit their final reports in July and August, but the bulk of the reports cannot be completed until September. Therefore, the total of all entitlements cannot be determined until final reports are processed under the various sections of the Act. It is necessary prior to receipt of all final reports to pay as much of the entitlement as possible and still leave a safe margin in case proration is required. This procedure enables applicant districts to receive promptly the major portion of entitlements due. 

Prorating payments on 1955 entitlements was found to be necessary after earlier estimates of requirements proved greater than the appropriated amounts. On the basis of best estimates made during the fiscal year, it seemed feasible to pay 100 percent of entitle- 10

ments. Thus initial payments on such entitlebe paid at 100 percent as required by subsecments of applicants were made at 75 percent on section 4 cases and at 90 percent on section 3 cases. As final reports were processed between June 30 and September 30, it became clear at this later date that the appropriation of $75 million would be inadequate by about $165,000 to pay 100 percent. The Commissioner therefore determined early in December to prorate payments on sections 2, 3, and 4 entitlements at 99.5 percent and notified applicants of this fact. Section 6 entitlements will tion 5(c).

When prorating is required, payments to applicant districts that failed to file timely a final report are, by regulation of the Commissioner, limited to the first payment; and to this payment only if a final report filed 30 days after the cut-off date substantiates the claim of the applicant. Twenty-nine applicants failed to file 

Form RSF-3, the final report, by September 30, and hence were penalized in this manner. 

RECENT AMENDMENTS 

Public Law 248 (83d Congress) provided amendments to the original Act (Public Law 874) that were to become effective in 1953-54 and 1954-55.

ELIGIBILITY BASE OF SECTION 3 BROADENED 

The major changes applicable to fiscal year 1955 under section 3 include the addition of two categories of children in the definition of eligibility; namely, (1) children living with parents employed on Federal property across State lines but within reasonable commuting distance, eligible under subsection 3(b); and (2) children living on Federal property with parents employed on Federal property across State lines but within reasonable commuting distance, or having a parent on active duty in the uniformed services, eligible under subsection 3(a).

ELIGIBILITY BASED ON PRECEDING YEAR'S AVERAGE DAILY ATTENDANCE 

Another major change was the provision for basing eligibility of subsection 3(a) and 3(b) pupils on the average daily attendance of the preceding fiscal year rather than on the current fiscal year. Under this provision the entitlements under section 3 for the 1954-55 school year were based for the first time on the preceding year's (1953-54) attendance data. 

ABSORPTION REQUIREMENT POSTPONED 

Public Law 732, approved August 31, 1954, postponed the required absorption by the applicant district (provided in P.L. 248) of 3 percent of the non-Federal pupils in average daily attendance.

ADDITIONAL PAYMENTS 

Public Law 248 also created the new subsection 3(c)(2) which permits additional payment to school districts in which 50 percent or more of the pupils in attendance during the preceding year were living on Federal property. Additional requirements under this subsection are a reasonable tax effort and a need for additional payment based on the premise that funds available from all sources are insufficient to provide a level of education equivalent to that maintained in comparable school districts.

PROVISIONS ELIMINATED 

Subsection 3(e) of the original Act, which provided additional payment for an increase in section 3 pupils in attendance not yet eligible for State aid, was eliminated by P.L. 248.

This provision was eliminated because payment on section 3 entitlement is now based on the preceding year's attendance.

Subsection 3(f) of the original Act provided aid for districts that had made preparation for pupils connected with Federal properties and that failed to realize the anticipated attendance of such children because of the cessation or decrease in the Federal activities on these properties. This provision was also no longer necessary under section 3 after the change was made to determine eligibility on the preceding year's attendance. Accordingly it was eliminated. However, Congress added a similar provision, subsection 4(d), to apply to subsection 4(a) impacts. 

Subsection 4(b), which also expired at the end of fiscal year 1954, was not extended by P.L. 248. This subsection covered increases resulting from Federal activity between 1939 and 1950. The original Act provided a 4-year declining entitlement for these wartime pupil increases that continued as a postwar burden on the school districts.

CHANGES IN BASE FOR 4(a) PAYMENTS 

Significant changes were made in provisions for determining eligibility with respect to children whose attendance was attributable to Federal activities carried on directly through contractors. Entitlement for 1954-55 under subsection 4(a), first-year assistance, was based on the increase over the preceding or 11

year in average daily attendance resulting from such activities in the current year, and on the increase in attendance during 1954-55 of children living on and/or living with parent working on Federal property. This amendment eliminated the eligibility of community-service children--those whose parents came into the district to provide services for the increased population resulting from Federal activities. The minimum number required for entitlement under this subsection for 195455 was 5 percent of the total average daily attendance during the prior year, but not more than the increase in total average daily attendance in 1954-55 over that of the preceding year. Previously the minimum requirement was 10 percent of the preceding 3-year average daily attendance.

Public Law 248 also amended subsection 4(a) of the original Act to reduce from 3 years to 2 years the period for which a school district may be eligible for payments on the basis of an increase occurring in a particular year. 

Under the amendment, a district that received a first-year entitlement under subsection 4(a) for fiscal year 1954 was entitled to a payment not to exceed 50 percent of the first-year payment if the need for these funds existed in the district the second year. 

Another important amendment by P.L. 248 to subsection 4(a) provided that eligibility of a district could be determined on the basis of an estimate. If the district did not meet the 5percent eligibility requirement at the end of the year and if the Commissioner had estimated that it would meet such requirement, the Federal payment would then be based on the number of eligible children in the district's schools. However, the original payment would not be taken away from the district because of its failure to meet the full 5-percent increase. This eliminated the difficulty under the original law caused by the delay in making payments to a heavily impacted district during a year when the funds were badly needed--a delay created by fear that the full eligibility requirements would not be reached and that the Federal payment made would have to be recaptured. to In 1954-55, entitlements under subsection 

4(a), first-year first-year assistance, amounted $2,746,526, compared with a total of $2,401,071 for 1953-54. The greater portion of the 1954-55 entitlement was payment for the increase in section 3 attendance during 1954-55 over that in 1953-54. Entitlements for subsection 4(a), second-year assistance, in 1954-55 amounted to $1,404,323.

ELECTION TO RECEIVE PAYMENTS WITH RESPECT TO INDIAN CHILDREN 

Section 10, P.L. 248, provides that the governor of a State may elect to have the local education agencies of the State receive P.L. 

874 payments with respect to Indian children attending the schools of the State in lieu of payments for educational services by the Bureau of Indian Affairs (under the Johnson- 

O'Malley Act). Only one State, Florida, elected to have the local agencies receive payments for Indian children under the provisions of P.L. 

874 for fiscal year 1955. 12

PROVIDING FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE FOR CONSTRUCTION OF SCHOOL FACILITIES UNDER TITLES II, III, AND IV, P.L. 815 PURPOSE AND PROVISIONS OF THE ACT 

Public Law 815 states that the policy of the United States Government is to provide financial assistance to local educational agencies for the construction of school facilities for federally caused increases in school membership. Originally, the program under P.L. 

815 was to operate until June 30, 1953, but it was modified and extended by P.L. 246 (August 

8, 1953) and again by P.L. 731 (August 31, 1954) until June 30, 1956.

The Act authorizes payment to be made to local educational agencies where there has been an increase in school membership of children residing on Federal property, with parents employed on Federal property; or where the increase in school membership has resulted from activities of the United States, carried on directly or through a contractor. 

The provisions of the Act are quite specific regarding the increase over a 2-year period in the number of such children in the applicant's schools to establish eligibility for aid and the amount of Federal assistance authorized. 

Subsection 305(a)(1) provides eligibility for children who reside on Federal property with parents who work on Federal property. Subsection 305(a)(2) provides eligibility either for children who live on Federal property or for children whose parents work on Federal property, but not both. The rate of payment for these children is less than it is for children who qualify under 305(a)(1). Both subsection 

305(a)1) and 305(a)(2) require a school district to have an increase of 20 or more eligible children, which increase must be at least 5 percent of the average daily membership during the school year 1953-54. The Federal property on which a claim is based must be in the same State as the applicant district or within reasonable commuting distance. 

Subsection 305(a)(3) provides eligibility to a school district for children whose attendance at school is the result of activities of the U.S. 

Government which are carried on directly by the Government or through a contractor. Among the conditions required for the purposes of subsection 305(a)(3) are: an increase of 20 eligible children and 10 percent of the total average daily membership of 1953-54; a showing that the construction of necessary facilities for these children would place an undue financial burden on the taxing and borrowing power of the district; and also a showing that part or all of the district is in an area in which the President finds that construction, reactivation or expansion of defense plants or military installations has caused a substantial in-migration of defense workers or military personnel. 

The maximum allowable grant under section 305 is determined by multiplying the number of eligible federally connected children for subsection 305(a)(1) by 95 percent of the average per-pupil cost of constructing minimum school facilities in the applicant's State; for subsection 305(a)(2) by 50 percent; and for subsection 305(a)(3) by 45 percent. 

To insure that districts will not be eligible for assistance unless they have more than a normal increase in school membership, Title III, subsection 305(d), imposed an additional requirement of growth in the number of nonfederally connected children. If the membership of the non-federally connected children in 

June 1956 is less than 110 percent of the average daily membership of these children in 1953-54, the difference must be deducted from the total number of all children who would otherwise be eligible for payment under subsection 305(a)(1), (2) and (3).

Section 304 of the Act limits Federal fi nancial assistance to the cost of constructing minimum school facilities for children who would otherwise be without such facilities at the close of the 1955-56 school year. The amount which may be allocated as a maximum grant is limited to the lesser of two amounts: one, the calculation of the amount allowable on the basis of the formula for increased "federally connected" children; and, two, the cost of constructing minimum school facilities for the "unhoused" children. Before Federal funds may be allotted to a local educational agency for the construction of school facilities, the Commissioner of Education must be assured that the applicant has or will have title to the site and the right to construct thereon the proposed school facilities, and will moreover maintain them for at least 20 years after completion of construction. The applicant must also have legal authority to undertake construction of the project and must be able to finance any non-Federal share as needed. Assurance must be given that work on the project will be commenced within a reasonable time and prosecuted to completion with diligence. The rates of pay for those engaged in the project will not be less than the prevailing local rates, determined by the Secretary of Labor in accord with the so-called Bacon-Davis Act. The local 14

agency must also assure the Commissioner facilities will be provided for the children of Education that the school facilities will be available to the federally connected children on the same terms, in accordance with State laws, as they are to other children enrolled and that the applicant will supply the Commissioner of Education with any information which he may reasonably require. In some instances, the membership increase which makes the district eligible for payment under section 305 is only temporary. 

In such cases, the school district may request the Commissioner (under section 309) to make the necessary temporary school facilities available at Federal expense. As an alternative, the school district may request the Commissioner to allot funds for permanent construction which would be equal to the amount necessary for temporary facilities. In the latter circumstances, the school district must assure the Commissioner that adequate school whose parents are federally connected. 

Some State laws preclude the extension of the State's educational system to children living on Federal property. Certain school districts, because of a lack of facilities or other difficulties resulting from isolated Federal posts, have been unable to provide suitable education for children living on Federal properties.

It was foreseen in P.L. 815 that conditions might arise where the local educational agency would not be able to provide school facilities for children living on Federal property. Therefore, under section 310, the Commissioner is given authority to provide or construct school facilities on Federal property in these situations. To the extent it is practicable, these school facilities are made comparable to facilities provided in comparable communities in the State.

APPROPRIATIONS 

Since the passage of the original Act in September 1950, the Congress has provided $609,000,000 for construction of schools in ten different appropriation Acts. (See table 

24.) The Atomic Energy Commission has turned over $96,000 to the Office of Education, making the total funds available for construction purposes $609,096,000 to date.

For Title II a total of $396,500,000, including the $55,000,000 provided for "unpaid entitlements" under P.L. 357, has been made available; and for Title III, $192,500,000. For

Title IV $20,000,000 has been made available to date.

ADMINISTRATION 

In the administration of this Act the Commissioner works with the local educational agencies through the State educational agencies--the several State Departments of Education--to coordinate the assistance given with State programs of education, and to receive advice and recommendations from State educational officials.

Public Law 815, as amended, requires, as a prerequisite to payment, that an application for aid from the local school district be submitted to the Commissioner through the appropriate State agency.

Subsection 209(a) authorizes the Commissioner to utilize the facilities and services of any Federal department or agency and to delegate the performance of his functions to any officer or employee of any Federal department or agency. This authority was given to avoid any duplication of staff and facilities in the Office of Education which might already exist in another Governmental agency. 

In the fiscal year 1951, and for each subsequent year, the Commissioner of Education arranged with the Administrator of Housing and Home Finance Agency for the Commis sioner of Community Facilities Administra tion to perform certain construction, engineering, fiscal and legal functions that are required in the administration of P.L. 815. This includes obtaining the assurances required by the Act, the examination of final plans and specifications, and the inspection and review of the progress of construction.

The function of constructing facilities under sections 203 and 204 in Title II, and under sections 309 and 310 of Title III, pursuant to applications approved by the Commissioner of Education, was also given the Commis sioner of Community Facilities Administration by authorizing him to prepare cost estimates for each project, arrange for preparation of plans and specifications, and supervise the construction of the projects. 

When P.L. 357 was passed, providing for the reimbursement of local expenditures against unpaid entitlements under Title II, the Commissioner of Community Facilities 

Administration obtained the necessary data concerning buildings constructed and date of contracts for processing these cases. 15

Under this working arrangement only a minimum staff, versed in educational administration, was required to be added to the Office of Education for this program. 

AVERAGE PER PUPIL COST OF CON- STRUCTING SCHOOL FACILITIES 

To determine the amount of the maximum grant under section 305, the "average per pupil cost" of constructing school facilities in each State had to be determined for school year 1953-54 for use in fiscal year 1955. Information was obtained from each State educational agency concerning the total cost, capacity, total floor area, area per pupil, cost per square foot, and cost per pupil of elementary schools and high schools constructed during the preceding year in each State. 

The computed figure representing the average cost per pupil of construction of school facilities was submitted to the respective State educational agencies for approval. These approved figures are shown by State in table 11. The figures used for fiscal year 1955 were based on data from the preceding fiscal year as required by subsection 210(7) of the Act. 

The rates for the 48 States shown in table 11 vary from $900 in 8 States to $1,600 in New York. The average is about $1,113. The average per-pupil cost in Alaska is $2,500 and in Puerto Rico it is $700.

RELATIVE URGENCY OF NEED AND PRIORITY 

When the funds available for any fiscal year are not sufficient to pay in full the maximum grants which all local educational agencies would otherwise receive, the Act now requires that the Commissioner, by regulation, shall prescribe an order of priority based on relative urgency of need for approval of applications timely filed.

It became evident from the data in the applications filed for fiscal year 1955 that sufficient funds were not available to pay, in full, the entitlements of all districts eligible under section 305. were issued re- 

Therefore, regulations quiring the use of a priority system based on relative urgency of need. Districts highest on the list, those in urgent need, received their allotments first.

The relative urgency of need of an applicant district is based on the percentage of membership determined to be eligible for payment, plus the percentage of membership in the applicant's schools determined to be unhoused, i.e., without the minimum school facilities. The latter percentage may not exceed the former.

Schools without minimum school facilities are those which must utilize available school facilities for more than the normal one session per day above the kindergarten level, or those schools that are dependent upon a building or space which is unsafe or, because of its character or inaccessibility, seems inappropriate to the educational objective. 

Federal funds are restricted to the cost of constructing minimum school facilities, thus assuring that Federal funds will be used to house the greatest number of eligible federally connected children.

UNPAID ENTITLEMENTS--PUBLIC LAW 357 

During the 1954 fiscal year, P.L. 357 appropriated $55 million to aid in the settlement of $99,285,269 of unpaid entitlements for school districts which had filed valid applications under section 202 subsequent to September 30, 1950, and on or before June 30, 1952.

The appropriation was to be used to pay not to exceed 70 percent of the unpaid entitlement of any district.

The Appropriation Act required the school districts to file renewal applications by October 1, 1954, and to submit "substitute" or "reimbursement" project applications by December 31, 1954, and the Commissioner to obligate all funds by April 1, 1955.

Public Law 246, authorizing this appropriation for unpaid entitlements, provided that the school district could not be reimbursed for construction of facilities for which contracts had been entered into prior to September 30, 1950. This provision reduced the unpaid entitlements by $6,873,000. 

By September 30, 1954, 537 renewal applications had been received; of these 503 were eligible. Of this number 122 were original reimbursement requests, and 381 were construction requests. These requests were approved for $54,812,137, which was 59.38 percent of each district's entitlement. One hundred eighty-five construction requests were transferred to reimbursements under the provisions of P.L. 357, making a total of 307 eligible reimbursement requests on which was paid a total of $31,443,311. Of the remaining 

196 construction requests, 29 were combined with other approved projects reducing the total construction and reimbursement projects to 474. These projects will provide an estimated 

2,175 classrooms with a total capacity of 

60,895 pupils. 

SUMMARY 

As of June 30, 1952, the end of the filing period under Title II of P.L. 815, 1, 125 school 

16

districts in 46 States, Alaska, and Hawaii had 

Hampshire, Vermont, and Wisconsin. The filed applications aggregating $392,488,339 in entitlements under section 202. Of these, 941 were found eligible, and 184 not eligible, for payment. No applications were received for construction projects from the States of Delaware and West Virginia. The total number of "federally connected" children in average daily attendance found eligible under subsection 

202(a) was 54,552, under 202(b) 398,135, and under 202(c) 173,965, or a total of 626,652 federally connected children out of a total enrollment of 2,954,427 children in districts eligible under Title II. (See 1954 Report, table 17.)

Out of this total enrollment it was estimated that 364,046 were unhoused even after counting existing facilities at 110 percent of capacity. 

As of August 31, 1955, 1,224 projects in 46 

States, Alaska, and Hawaii had been approved for a total of $291,403,117 in Federal funds.

This will provide 14,006 classrooms and related facilities to house 408,554 pupils. (See tables 21 and 22.) Of these projects 1,139 had been completed, while 84 were still under construction, and payment in the amount of $286,749,643 had been made on these projects. (See table 22.)

Under section 305 of P.L. 815, as amended by P.L. 246, 672 applications had been filed as of August 31, 1955. These requests came from 45 States, Alaska, Puerto Rico, and

Hawaii and were based on figures from the 

1954 fiscal year. Of this number 445 applications were found to be eligible and 227 were declared ineligible. No applications were received from districts in North Dakota, Vermont, and West Virginia. The number of eligible pupils in the subsection 305(a)(1) cate gory was 17,921, the 305(a)(2) was 127,653, and the 305(a)(3) was 29,794, or a total of 175,368 federally connected children out of a total enrollment of 2,204,904. (See table 14.)

Under section 305, P.L. 815, as amended by 

P.L. 731, as of August 31, 1955, 618 applications had been received from 41 States, Alaska, and Hawaii for fiscal year 1955. Of this number 340 applications were found to be eligible and 278 were found to be ineligible.

No applications were received from the States of Louisiana, Maine, Minnesota, Montana, New

Title IV authorizes aid to districts which offer free public education to large numbers of children (principally Indian) who live on tax-exempt Federal property but are ineligible under Title II and Title III because there has been no substantial increase in the numbers of these pupils. The number claimed must represent a substantial percentage of the total number of children for whom free public education number of eligible pupils in the subsection 

305(a)(1) category was 7,399, the 305(a)(2) was 

85,230, and the 305(a)(3) was 15,089, or a total of 107,718 federally connected children out of a total enrollment of 1,432,894. (See table 15.)

There were 2,096 projects under sections 

202 and 305 approved as of August 31, 1955, for construction of facilities which it is estimated will provide 22,727 classrooms to house 

670,375 pupils. (See table 16.) 

As of August 31, 1955, $158,182,056 had been reserved for 934 projects entitled to receive aid under section 305. These projects were located in 43 States, Alaska, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico. It is estimated that these projects will provide 7,708 classrooms and related facilities and will house 230,359 pupils. (See table 21.) Of these, 389 were under construction and 88 had been completed. Payments in the amount of $74,925,096 had been made on these projects. (See table 22.)

Twelve applications were received for construction of temporary facilities under section 

309. (See table 21.) Funds were reserved in the amount of $1,685,430 for these 12 projects.

There were one project in Idaho, one in Nevada, three in Missouri, three in Ohio, one in 

Oregon and three in Washington. These temporary projects will provide 80 classrooms to house 2,780 children. Payments in the amount of $1,327,455 had been made as of the date of this report. 

Under section 310, 75 applications were received from 18 States, Alaska, and Puerto

Rico for construction of school facilities on Federal property at an estimated cost of approximately $12,000,000. Funds in the amount of $10,352,387 have been reserved for 44 projects to provide 236 classrooms and related facilities to house 7,406 pupils as of 

August 31, 1955. Table 18 lists by State the 

Federal installations involved, the number of pupils to be provided for, and the Federal funds reserved for the individual projects. As of August 31, 1955, 5 of the 44 projects had been completed and 39 were under construction, and payments in the amount of $4,142,900 had been made from Federal funds. 

TITLE IV 

is provided. ("Substantial percentage" has been defined by regulation as 10 percent.) 

It also must be shown that the immunity of the Federal property to taxation has created a substantial and continuing impairment to the ability of the school district to finance needed school facilities; that the applicant is making a reasonable tax effort and is taking the oppor- 17

tunity to use State and other funds available for this purpose; and that this educational agency does not have sufficient funds from all other sources to provide the needed minimum school facilities.'

In the two fiscal years 1954 and 1955, appropriations were authorized for payment under Title IV. The aggregate appropriations were limited to $20 million and no agreement could be made extending assistance after June 

30, 1955. 

1 The 84th Congress, 1st Session, made three significant changes in

Title IV in the passage of P.L. 382: 

(1) It extended the approval date for applications by one fiscal year, to June 30, 1956. (2) It permits a district which lacks "a substantial percentage" of pupils who live on Indian lands to be eligible if there are 100 or more of these pupils. (3) It permits the Commissioner to waive the clause under subsection 401(2) which requires that the immunity of such property to taxation has created a substantial and continuing impairment of its ability to finance needed school facilities for a district providing free public education for pupils who reside on Indian lands outside the district, Payments can be made only after an application has been submitted by the local school districts, through State educational agencies, to the Commissioner of Education in accord with regulations prescribed by him. 

As of June 30, 1955, the closing date for obligating funds under the Act, 112 applications had been filed under Title IV. These applications came from 20 States and Alaska, requesting 133 projects. At that time 80 applications were found eligible and 32 were found ineligible or were transferred to Title III applications.

A total of $16,102,900 had been reserved on 91 projects for an estimated 650 classrooms to house 18,645 pupils. Sixteen projects were approved for other facilities such as multipurpose rooms, heating plants, and auxiliary facilities of various kinds. (See tables 21 and 23.)

As of August 31, 1955, 6 projects had been completed and 49 were under construction. 

Payments amounting to $7,433,893 had been made to the applicants. (See table 22.) 18
